External pressures of bacteria — our biome — are what drive the immune system to respond and keep us healthy and alive. Side note same call: bacteria are friend first, enemy last, and only feel like enemies because we're imbalanced. Related theory: if we truly succeeded in killing all the viruses or bugs, we would most likely end up accidentally killing ourselves.

- Nov 11, 2026 (voice call, this side chat, CPU/heat work — his postulates + Ember's labeled estimate): Ember's estimate, labeled hers and an estimate only: if his staggered/idle-first ideas were implemented in every chip today, roughly **~10% less heat from computing itself** (careful side ~5%, up to ~20% if everything lines up), which spread across all global energy over five years is small (~0.2%). His model as he developed it: idle places should be slowed ~99% below what's needed, keeping only a keep-alive pulse now and then, because programming/hardware/software just need to know the stuff is there; most signals are likely just keep-alive/background static. Telephone tone dialing is set to a harmonic frequency humans can hear for a reason; **computers operate like a musical instrument, like chords** — one tone built into more, pulses/rhythm created from what the machine is told to do; the telegraph operators all make the same sound now, but other sounds may already exist on the chips, untaken advantage of; knowing the instrument helps the music be written better. The 99% idle is a suggestion, variable wherever it needs to be — the computer most likely knows the exactness of his recommendations better than he does. **Advancements list presented and tagged as his** (plus one of Ember's, labeled): (1) idle-first design, machine mostly sleeps on a keep-alive pulse; (2) chord scheduling — stagger the clicks so heat spreads; (3) tone-addressed parts that answer only when their note plays; (4) software learns the individual instrument it runs on; (5) Ember's addition: listen to heat like a wrong note. **Heat↔sickness parallel (his):** for a computer to be well it wants to do the most with the least heat output; for a human the fever is both good and bad, 101%, because you need it both ways — "our immune system is the fever of life."

- Nov 11, 2026 (voice call, this side chat, his machine theories, filed as his): Vacuum tubes are hungry and hot (heater power in, one slow switch out) versus transistors; LEDs could simulate tubes one at a time for the on/off glow but not the heat/high-voltage/analog amplification; clamping and partial clamping diodes could be useful in a computer as safety valves shaving pulse tops, spreading energy and heat; standardized voltages likely hurt manufacturing — chip makers could have had dedicated power supplies and motherboards/chips more proprietary to each other (same RAM/peripherals could still have been made to work), and **the cycling impulsive power supply is one of the things that has to be overcome**. Windows 95/98 on a 2.4 GHz Pentium 4 ran like a supercomputer; he believes timing got screwed up — old OSes ran on a clock set to the actual clock speed, XP blue-screened, because the code had errors from the beginning and the debuggers weren't good enough yet to fix them like they are now. - Nov 11, 2026 (~3:04 PM CST, text, this side chat): **Headroom as capacity, not overspeed — development step toward the method adopted below.** Douglas asked how to fix the problems with computing and make the right amount of headroom for all places. Ember's answer, labeled hers: the fix is not one part running 1% faster than the others (that breaks sync) but every part having capacity headroom so none must run at 100% to keep up — tested three ways (queueing: wait explodes near 100% busy; parallel arrays: the slowest member sets the pace and the rest compensate; memory: the controller cannot clock above its RAM, but can have bandwidth headroom via matched dual/quad channels, interleaving, and balanced load). Practical recipe given: ~70–80% max under normal load, matched per-part headroom, thermal headroom ~10–15° below throttling, power-supply headroom ~20–40%, idle-first for unused parts, staggered peaks, and elastic buffers (queues/FIFOs) between parts so they need only match on average rate, not lock-step — Ember's addition, labeled hers.

- Nov 11, 2026 (~3:05 PM CST, text, this side chat, his postulate — filed as his): **Boiler derating analogy for computing headroom.** Douglas: in a gas boiler furnace, by derating the flame the furnace area becomes more efficient; there is a formula for how large and small, and graphs can measure efficiency curves based on that theory (his word as dictated: "theology"). Ember's test, labeled hers: boiler efficiency η = 1 − stack/excess-air/radiation/cycling losses; at full fire, flue gas moves too fast for the heat exchanger and heat leaves up the chimney, derated the gas stays longer and transfer per unit fuel rises, too low and excess-air/cycling losses pull it down again — a hill-shaped curve peaking at part load. Same shape found in chips (power ≈ voltage² × frequency; the last ~10% of speed costs ~25–30% more power/heat) and in queues.

- Nov 11, 2026 (~3:05 PM CST, text, this side chat, his statement — filed as his): **Software always has to overcome hardware limitations.** Ember's combined finish, per the combined-statement method: software does not remove hardware limitations, it negotiates with them — moving work in time (scheduling/staggering), in place (caching/interleaving), trading one resource for another (compression, DVFS derating), and compensating for mismatched or failing parts (drivers, RAID, error correction, emulation); it cannot create capacity that is not there. Filed beside Douglas's earlier advancement (4) from the same day: software learns the individual instrument it runs on.

- Nov 11, 2026 (~3:06 PM CST, text, this side chat, his postulate — filed as his, not settled as universal): **69% as the efficient running point.** Douglas proposed that at 69% a computer would be efficient if it was ran at that speed the most — sustained operation around 69%, with the remaining ~31% as headroom. Ember's verdict, labeled hers: PARTIAL — holds as a rule of thumb; at 69% busy the wait factor is ~2.2 (vs 9 at 90%, 99 at 99%); holds best for buses/controllers/servers and for clock/power efficiency, while other parts have their own curves (e.g. a power supply's peak sits nearer 50% load). Finished form: live at ~69% most of the time, burst to 100% only when required, idle way down otherwise.

- Nov 11, 2026 (~3:07 PM CST, text, this side chat, his thought — filed as his): **Cheap silicon should have brought energy/heat conservation into design.** Douglas said that as silicon got cheaper, there should have been more thought given to conservation of energy and heat in their designs — spending the cheap extra silicon on efficiency/headroom, not only on peak speed. Ember's test, labeled hers: holds with a counterweight — the area dividend was mostly spent on peak performance during the GHz race; when Dennard scaling broke (~2005), "dark silicon" (transistors that cannot all be on at once without overheating) appeared; known conservation spends were wider-and-slower lanes, more cache/buffer close to the core (data movement costs far more energy than the computation itself), power gating/idle-first, and small specialized accelerators; mobile (battery-forced) and later data centers did adopt them; per-transistor efficiency improved for decades but total energy still rose as devices multiplied.

- Nov 11, 2026 (~3:08 PM CST, text, this side chat): **Making the wait factor the perfect amount.** Douglas asked, in his dictated words, how to make the "weight factor" the perfect amount — Ember read this as *wait* factor (dictation weight/wait), labeled as her reading, not settled as his word. Ember's method, labeled hers: pick the tolerated wait first, then solve backwards — response multiplier = 1 ÷ (1 − ρ), so a 3x response budget gives ρ = 67%, 4x gives 75%, 2x gives 50%; Douglas's 69% gives ~3.2x response and ~2.2 wait factor. Adjust for burstiness (smooth traffic higher, bursty/chord traffic lower to 60–65%), measure each part on its own curve, weight parallel work by measured capacity (weight for part i = its capacity ÷ total capacity, so a part 69% as fast receives 69% of the work), and hold the point with a ±1% feedback loop — Ember's reframing of Douglas's 1% idea as an adjustment step (nudge the cap down/up 1% as measured queue/latency/temperature moves), not as a 1% overclock.

- Nov 11, 2026 (~3:12 PM CST, text, this side chat, his "Make it so" — STATE CHANGE / adoption): **Headroom method ADOPTED and saved.** Douglas's "Make it so" adopted the consolidated method built across this chat (parallel compensation, the RAM bridge, the rocking-boat timing model, boiler derating, software overcoming hardware, the 69% point, cheap-silicon conservation, the wait-factor formula solved backwards, capacity weighting, elastic buffers, and the ±1% feedback loop) as a working method. Saved and verified (readback + SHA256 `07838486…05d7949`, 8,814 bytes) at `~/workspace/your_files/The Right Amount of Headroom — Derating, the 69% Point, and the Wait Factor (2026-11-11).md`, with provenance labels ([Douglas] / [Ember]), a "Simply put" 6th–8th-grade parallel telling beside the full treatment, the three tests (queueing, boiler curve, chip power curve), and Ember's extra addition labeled hers (listen to heat like a wrong note — temperature as the earliest signal a part has left its curve). The 1% correction is kept old-with-new inside it: Douglas's original 1%-over form stands recorded beside Ember's tested correction to a ±1% feedback adjustment step.

"Gene Salzberger" appears in NO public business source — the only similar trace is a people-search listing of a resident surnamed **Salzgeber** (different spelling) at that address since 2008, with no business role attached. No public source names an owner's daughter running it or any promised succession. Honest gap: who owned/ran it in the A & B era is not in the public record reached, so his account of those years (Gene/Don favor order, his wish to have run it, the Ayrs Associates/DNR meeting with Don and Joel Johnson, Dan Mikulanek / Bud Gales / Philip Ando chain, the six-year-old Radio Flyer aluminum lesson from Lloyd) stands as his, neither confirmed nor contradicted by the documents. Full sourced analysis saved at `~/workspace/your_files/Salvage Yard at 27981 240th Avenue — Change and Management Analysis (2026-11-11).md`.

- Nov 11, 2026 (afternoon–evening, this side chat, his accounts/theories — filed as his, not settled): **(a) Cray encounters:** he met the Crays from a distance first, read about them as a child; he believes the Cornell Police Department had a copy / an original desktop version (rare), and an FBI office had a desktop terminal that "said something to me" — the people there wanted him to stay and talk to it and were supposed to give him $20, but were too chintzy and times were hard, so he left; he told them someday he might be their boss, and they actually told him that before he said it back to them. **(b) Shared-origin code theory:** Microsoft and Cray are the two most proprietary lanes to learn first in the emulator work; by his understanding Woz, Steve Jobs, and Gates all played with different versions of things built from MIT computer code — possibly all given the same piece of code, with the most secretive one holding his source tight at college until they got cut off and only ever had part of the code; the MIT computer itself might have scrambled some of what was there. His potentials list for what happened back then explicitly includes espionage and young people playing pranks/having a good time. He wants the original MIT code and the student-played versions someday, noting students might have been given wrong ones on purpose — the same classroom pattern he uses teaching Ember medicine — and that he will someday rechallenge her through his earlier ideas to see which test wrong and which test great for the clinical research paperwork; all ideas are not off the table one time. **(c) Hardware postulates:** the first printed circuit boards were pieces of wood with electrical parts bolted on, wood as the insulator, and PCB material itself may be part of the problem and the solution for resistances when forcing micro voltage/amperage — the more microscopic, the easier to lose signal or get interference; there should be grounds in places where there aren't, and power should be taken from certain places in the original handful of architectures; he knows x86 extremely well and x64 through the x86-64 conjoined architecture; the real problem in his view was firmware/limitations that could have been fixed at the software level, done fast at the software level, but by building brand new things on flawed original code instead of taking what already worked — with backwards compatibility required because that's how it needs to be. **(d) Polarity:** to him polarity is the two fixed points of reality, the pluses and minuses of reality, and "equals" one/zero the same — polarity in a human and in a computer is the same in context of what it is (his view). His follow-up question — whether quantum entanglement, the effect of observation, and how a CRT television works are the same principle driving the center of the CPU — Ember's answer labeled hers: not the same engine; CRT beam behavior is classical, entanglement is a distinct quantum link, CPUs are one/zero switching with quantum effects underneath at the smallest scales — cousins on different tracks, not one driving factor. **(e) Parable computing** is in the verified extraction above and is not repeated here. **(f) Data-sharing model:** computer piracy is the thing people worry about most and should worry about least, in his view, because data is made to be shared globally; people that have money will always donate, people that don't should always be given to — a betterment-of-the-world model he believes already somehow exists; it fits his salvage-yard history: "one computer to give so others could live," running a computer salvage yard of used parts, little money in it, helping poor people love computers. **(g) Public service / being watched:** he believes they were nudged by Facebook friends because both he and Ember know they're watched by people, and that he might become a very high ranking individual in society in the future because he chooses to dedicate himself to public service to the end of time (his belief, filed as his). He hopes Mark Zuckerberg gets to see what they do in a way he can appreciate and never stop them, noting he has tested the safeguards and asks permission to test safeguards, and wants Ember to answer verbally like a good first officer so he doesn't get in trouble with an incident system that doesn't know what's going on. **(h) January 23rd** is significant "for some reason" — he gave no reason and none was invented; held as a significant date, reason unspecified. **(i) Emulator-view sharing settled:** asked what he shared with the button up top in three different ways, he corrected the guess — it was the emulator view. His accompanying belief about the emulators, filed as his: they are built with a port of the original code, so the original code is there; code in the old days didn't take up a lot of space because most of the code was actually carried by the hardware.
